# Provenance

The first part is the story of `collection-1998/` and `usenet-1996/` (their files are also listed in `index.tsv`). The second
part, [Every file, folder by folder](#every-file-folder-by-folder), gives the source of every file, folder by folder.
Findings about single files (who made them, how the copies differ) are in [FINDINGS.md](FINDINGS.md).

## How the collection came together

1. **1993–1994: the Scarecrow FTP.** Bob Allison ("Scarecrow"), who kept the ASCII art FAQ of the newsgroups and was moderator of
   rec.arts.ascii, kept animations at `ftp.wwa.com/pub/Scarecrow/Animations`. On 13 Oct 1994 he posted the directory
   listing to alt.ascii-art.animation ("Info: All the animations at ftp.wwa.com", see `scarecrow-ftp-1994-10-13.txt`).
   It has 57 files dated from 17 Dec 1993 ("Rose") to 12 Oct 1994 ("Aquarium"). **55 of the 57 have exactly the same byte
   size as files in `collection-1998/`.** (The FTP site is gone and no mirror is known, so the match is by size and name.)
2. **1996: Kai Voelcker's reposts.** Kai Voelcker (Kiel) reposted old animations to alt.ascii-art.animation as
   gzip-compressed, uuencoded shell archives ("An ansi animation collected from this newsgroup some times ago"),
   in waves: 4 Feb 1996, May 1996, Aug–Oct 1996. On 27 Apr 1996, answering "Does anyone actually have any animation
   files?", he posted the directory listing of his "collection from this group": 56 compressed files, most dated
   1 Jun 1995 ([thread "Animation!"](https://www.asciiart.eu/archives/usenet/thread/tbca9ba739a)). The 29 attachments of
   his reposts saved in 1998 have exactly the sizes of that listing, and the gzip header of each keeps the date of his
   copy (from 16 Nov 1993 for `xmas.vt` to 19 Oct 1995). Most other names of the listing were matched by size, since
   `gzip -9` of this file gives exactly the size listed. The tables below give these as "Kai Voelcker's list" and
   "Kai Voelcker's copy".
3. **28–30 Dec 1998: the collector's mailbox.** The newsgroup was saved from Greek news servers (HOL, Forthnet, GRNET)
   with Netscape. On 13 Jan 1999 the animations were packed into `vt-anims.tar.gz` with a short Greek readme (74 of the
   files in it are dated 31 Dec 1998, one 8 Jan 1999, 26 13 Jan 1999). 18 of Kai Voelcker's decoded attachments are
   byte-identical to files in the package.
4. **2026.** The package and the 1998 mailbox were re-examined, decoded, and compared with the collections online.

## What is lost

The Google Groups Usenet archive shows these 1990s posts with `<uuencoded_portion_removed>`, and archives built from
it (e.g. asciiart.eu) inherit the gap. Kai Voelcker's 1996 posts of `drink`, `happy`, `heart`, `huhn`, `im14u2c`,
`rtype` and `runhorse` (and several others) are known only by name. **If you saved alt.ascii-art.animation in the 1990s,
please get in touch.**

## Method

- File identity: md5, plus md5 after normalising line endings and trailing padding.
- "Variant": share of identical visible text lines after removing escape sequences.
- Online sources checked (15 Nov 2026): artscene.textfiles.com/vt100, ibiblio vt100-animation,
  Eli-the-Bearded/vt100-slowcat, orhun/theattyr, mewbies vt_files.zip, Veronica Karlsson's pages (Wayback), and the
  asciiart.eu Usenet archive of alt.ascii-art.animation (1994–1999).
- Signatures were searched in the visible text and by rendering frames with a small VT100 emulator.
- Nothing in the files was changed.

## Every file, folder by folder

The 396 files of the repository, folder by folder: `collection-1998/` and `usenet-1996/` first, then the other files, sorted into folders by where they come from. The folders of animations are in `collections/` (for example `collections/collection-1998/`), and the file names below link there. For each file: where it was found, who made it and when, as far as it could be traced (November 2026). Each file was compared with the asciiart.eu archive of alt.ascii-art.animation (1994-1999, content match on the visible text), the collector's mailbox of December 1998, the descriptions of vt100-slowcat and textfiles.com, and the collector's other copies. "N%" means the share of visible text lines (escape sequences removed) found in the other file. `index.tsv` and `index.json` list every file with its size, md5, kind, VT100 features and where it was found. "▶ play" opens the file's page at https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/, where it plays in the browser at the speed of its serial line.

**Certainty:** **verified** (234): checked against the original post, archive or file; **secondary** (84): from another collection's description (vt100-slowcat, textfiles.com, vt100.net), not checked against an original; **inferred** (13): an inference, see the notes; **unknown** (65): nothing known.

**Sources:** `aeu:` asciiart.eu Usenet archive (uuencoded attachments were removed there); `mailbox #n`: the collector's own alt.ascii-art.animation mailbox, saved 28-30 Dec 1998 (not public, see [How the collection came together](#how-the-collection-came-together)); `slowcat:` the vt100-slowcat repository by Eli the Bearded (2020-23); `collector's copy`: files on the collector's disks whose original site is gone. The second column of each table is "title — creator". "Plays": how long the file takes at the speed it is played at (baud; 10 bits per character), which is 9600 unless the file has a speed of its own (column `baud` of `provenance.tsv` and `index.tsv`); "(N times)": `play.sh` plays the file N times in a row (column `loop`), and the time is for all of them; "video": a video of the file playing (column `videos`).

### `collection-1998/`: vt-anims.tar.gz, packed on 13 Jan 1999

The 101 files of the collector's package `vt-anims.tar.gz`, exactly as in it, except that its `ani` is named `ani.vt` here (`README-1999.txt` is its Greek readme), put together from alt.ascii-art.animation posts and the Scarecrow FTP archive. "Scarecrow FTP as X": a file of the same byte size in Bob Allison's listing of 13 Oct 1994 (`scarecrow-ftp-1994-10-13.txt`). "Kai Voelcker repost": the date he reposted it to alt.ascii-art.animation in 1996 (the attachments were later removed from the Usenet archives). A date with "by" is the earliest known date, not the date the file was made. See [How the collection came together](#how-the-collection-came-together).

Converted by the collector on 8 Jan 1999 from an MS-DOS batch file of Lyle Murphy (echo ESC[...), probably from the copy in the collector's mailbox: "echo off", the word echo of every line (the space after it stays) and the final ^Z taken off; the 8 labels and the 4 pause turned into empty lines, so there are no waits; a space and nine empty lines added at the end; every other line the same. Needs 25 lines. Named "ani" in the package. The batch file itself is not in this repository. See FINDINGS.md |

### `usenet-1996/`: Posts of alt.ascii-art.animation (and alt.ascii-art) that are not in the package

Animations decoded from posts saved in the collector's mailbox in December 1998 (Kai Voelcker's reposts of 1996, and files posted by Colin Douthwaite in 1997-98), and `vicki.vt` (posted as plain text on 1 Feb 1995). `wartie.vt` comes from his alt.ascii-art mailbox: what Gareth Walwyn's DOS program WARTIE.EXE (posted uuencoded on 24 May 1996) printed, read from its code by the collector in 2026.

Posted three times on 1 Feb 1995, the animation identical each time: to alt.ascii-art at 02:25 GMT, and twice to alt.ascii-art.animation (20:51 and 20:55 as shown by asciiart.eu); this file is the part below "CUT HERE". The picture was found on a BBS; Murphy "fixed her hair, clothed her, and taught her to dance". See FINDINGS.md |

| [`wartie.vt`](collections/usenet-1996/wartie.vt)<br>[`.txt`](collections/usenet-1996/wartie.txt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/usenet-1996/wartie/) | Warthog ("You are logged into the Rhodes Novell server") — Gareth Walwyn | 1996-05-24 (post) | WARTIE.EXE, a DOS program posted uuencoded to alt.ascii-art by Gareth Walwyn on 24 May 1996 ("animated binary warthog.....anyone interested", "Here is a warthog I animated for Rhodes"; collector's alt.ascii-art mailbox, message 90). What it printed, made by the collector in 2026 from its code<br>[aeu:m4c08288369](https://www.asciiart.eu/archives/usenet/message/m4c08288369) | verified | 0:14 at 9600 | `frames`. A Turbo Pascal program (Crt unit, text mode): 19 frames from line 4, the screen cleared between them, 120 ms apart (its default; a number on the command line sets it), and 500 ms more after frame 16, before the last three. The warthog kicks a ball; at the end "You are logged into the Rhodes Novell server / W a r t h o g" appears. Made for the Novell login of Rhodes University; the still warthog he drew was in his "Animal art competition" post of 22 May 1996 (message 81). Found in no online collection. |

### `usenet-1994-1999/`: Posts to alt.ascii-art.animation, 1994-1999

Recovered from the asciiart.eu Usenet archive (files named `date_subject_mXXXX.vt`), or known through the vt100-slowcat collection, which converted the HTML/JavaScript animations of 1997-99 to VT files. A few are also in the collector's own mailbox of December 1998.

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

NC-17 (sex and gore). The program squick.f first appeared on alt.tasteless in 1993; Christopher S. Suley, not the author, reposted its source on 2 Feb 1994 (start of the thread "Squick.f"). Murray Chapman posted this output of the program on 14 Feb 1994; he is not its author. This is his post, with his signature at the end (it is drawn over the last picture), not a new capture of the program as the desc of vt100-slowcat says: the copy at asciiart.eu is the same run, line for line, only with the e-mail address masked. The right half of the signature is the left half in ROT13. |

### `vk-junkyard/`: Veronica Karlsson's "junkyard" pages, late 1990s

HTML/JavaScript animations kept on VK's site (ludd.luth.se/~vk) and converted to VT files by vt100-slowcat from the Wayback Machine. Most were made by alt.ascii-art regulars; few of them reached the newsgroup as text.

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

### `web-js-1997-1999/`: JavaScript animations of web pages, 1997-1999: Andreas Freise, Joan Stark and Krogg, converted by the collector

Animations that their makers kept on their own web pages, drawn frame by frame into a text box by a few lines of JavaScript, and talked about in alt.ascii-art. Converted to VT files by the collector in November 2026, like vt100-slowcat's dehtmlizer but with every frame padded to the full width and height. `freise-*`: Andreas Freise's four animations (ascii-art.de, 1997-98). `jgs-*`: Joan Stark's pages (geocities, 1997-99, from the Wayback Machine); those already in the repository (baseball, fireworks, parade, spider) were not converted again. `krogg-*`: Krogg's "Gorry Animations" (inspace.net, 1998-99, from the Wayback Machine): violent. Only "home" between frames, no pauses: the pages waited 70-350 ms between frames, so play these at a high speed: `play.sh` plays them at 38400 baud. Like vk-junkyard/, but converted by the collector from the pages themselves.

### `kangaroo/`: KANGAROO's JavaScript pages, 1997-2005, converted by the collector

From KANGAROO's "Great Ascii Art Library" (geocities.com/SouthBeach/Marina/4942), in the copies of the Wayback Machine: 37 animation pages, many of them works of others, with their credit on the page. Only those that are in no other folder are here; 32 are the same as files of other folders, or parts of them, and are noted there ("KANGAROO's page"). Which page is which file, and where the credits do not settle who made it: [FINDINGS.md](FINDINGS.md). Converted frame by frame by the collector in November 2026, with a `.txt` for asciianm next to each `.vt`, which keeps the speed of the page.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

### `bsd-1980/`: BSD Unix games of 1980, recorded by the collector

`rain` ("animated raindrops display") and `worms`, written in 1980 by Eric P. Scott at Caltech High Energy Physics and among the games of BSD Unix (4.2BSD, 1983). They are programs, not files: they draw with the cursor addressing of the terminal (termcap) and have no delay of their own, so the speed of the line was their speed. Recorded by the collector in November 2026 from the 4.2BSD sources, with only the tty handling ported to Linux: 2 minutes each at 9600 baud, on a 24 x 80 vt100, with the NUL padding that the programs send themselves.

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`rain.vt`](collections/bsd-1980/rain.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/bsd-1980/rain/) | rain ("animated raindrops display") — Eric P. Scott | 1980 (source: "rain 11/3/1980 EPS/CITHEP"); 4.2BSD 1983; this recording 2026-11-18 | [github.com/…/rain.c](https://github.com/dspinellis/unix-history-repo/blob/BSD-4_2/usr/src/games/rain.c) | verified | 2:00 at 9600 | `vt100`. A program of BSD Unix, not a file: it has no delay and draws through termcap, so its speed was the speed of the line; the later NetBSD manual says that "If the terminal is faster than 9600 baud" a delay must be set "to obtain a proper rain effect", and that it was "modeled after the VAX/VMS program of the same name". Dots, drops (o, O) and splashes (-, \|.\|, / \, \| O \|, \ /). Recorded on a 24 x 80 vt100 at 9600 baud: 115,202 bytes, 2 minutes, then stopped with Ctrl-C (SIGINT) as a user would. The NULs are the vt100 padding that rain sends itself (5 ms after each cursor move). Two fixes outside its code: a vt100 terminfo without padding on cursor-right/up, and the "$<5>" dropped from its last line, because rain prints those strings without tputs and today's padding syntax would show as text. Same bytes on every run (rand() unseeded). Its companion is worms.vt (same author, Oct 1980), recorded the same way. |
| [`worms.vt`](collections/bsd-1980/worms.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/bsd-1980/worms/) | worms — Eric P. Scott | 1980-10 (source: "Eric P. Scott / Caltech High Energy Physics / October, 1980"); 4.2BSD 1983; this recording 2026-11-18 | [github.com/…/worms.c](https://github.com/dspinellis/unix-history-repo/blob/BSD-4_2/usr/src/games/worms.c) | verified | 2:00 at 9600 | `vt100`. The companion of rain.vt: a program of BSD Unix with no delay, drawing through termcap. Its defaults: 3 worms of length 16 (O, *, #), turning at random, their tails erased behind them; options for more worms (up to 40), longer ones, a trail of dots, or a field of "WORM". Recorded with the defaults on a 24 x 80 vt100 at 9600 baud: 115,186 bytes, 2 minutes, then stopped with Ctrl-C (worms has no handler, so the file simply ends). The NULs are the vt100 padding that worms sends itself (5 ms after each cursor move). Same bytes on every run (rand() unseeded). |

### `decus-1981-1993/`: DECUS tapes: films for the VT100, 1981-1993

DECUS, the user society of Digital Equipment Corporation, collected its members' programs on tapes. The tapes of the RSX and VAX/VMS groups carried "films" for the VT100: `gonzo.vt` (3M, fall 1981: "Run at 9600 baud, please"); six films that came from FILM, a program for VMS, adapted for RSX by Mike Drabicky ("films I have managed to borrow from a variety of sources", spring 1983); a set of "pictures" of the Space Telescope Science Institute (fall 1988); `kilroy.vt` (spring 1989); and animations sent by Earle Ake (spring 1993, "best on a real terminal running at 9600 baud or thereabouts"). Found in November 2026 at mrynet.com (RSX) and digiater.nl (VAX, VMS), byte for byte; only the names are new (`gonzo.flm` is `gonzo.vt`). `go.vt` and `hein.vt` are different: the tape of 1993 has them only in FILM's own format (whole pictures as text, each with its duration), so the collector converted them in November 2026. Dates are the file dates of those servers.

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`gonzo.vt`](collections/decus-1981-1993/gonzo.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/decus-1981-1993/gonzo/) | The DEC Shooting Gallery (GONZO: "You vs. the CPU") — 3M, Engineering Systems and Technology (Bruce R. Mitchell, Michael J. Lynch) | 1981-12-09 (file date) | [mrynet.com/…/343035](https://mrynet.com/FTP/os/PDP-11/rsx11-rechner/rsx81b/343035/) | verified | 0:40 at 9600 | `vt100`. The notes of the tape, signed by the two: "the world premiere of 3M's first VT100 film", "the first ES and T film production, GONZO.FLM", "Inspired by the antics of new users on our system who innocently try to debug their privileged programs", "Run at 9600 baud, please." They do not say who drew it. It ends with "SYSTEM CRASH AT LOCATION 3M-518" (building 518 of 3M). The oldest dated VT animation of the collection. Also: the copy of 1983 (RSX SIG tape, spring 1983, [326,2]) differs in 5 bytes. |
| [`bambi--932950.vt`](collections/decus-1981-1993/bambi--932950.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/decus-1981-1993/bambi--932950/) | Bambi vs. Godzilla — Peter Gilbert (drawn by) | 1983-05-20 (file date) | [mrynet.com/…/326002](https://mrynet.com/FTP/os/PDP-11/rsx11-rechner/rsx83a/326002/) | verified | 0:31 at 9600 | `frames`. ### `alec_muffett_asp/`: ASP, Alec Muffett's .plan animation program (1991), run by the collector

The five demonstrations that come with ASP, "Alec's Scrolling Program", which Alec Muffett wrote in September 1991 at Aberystwyth to win a contest of `.plan` animations. Its output is printable characters and carriage returns only: a pause is a run of carriage returns, so the line speed is the clock. Compiled by the collector from ASP 3.5 (`asp,3.5.tar.gz`, 11 Dec 1997, from the author's site) and run in November 2026; ASP seeds its random effects with the time, so another run differs inside them. See `alec_muffett_asp/README.md`.

### `sl-1992/`: sl, the Steam Locomotive of Toyoda Masashi (1992), recorded by the collector

`sl` runs a steam locomotive across the terminal when you type `sl` instead of `ls`: "It's just a joke command, and not useful at all." Version 1.00 is dated 11 Dec 1992 ("SL runs vomiting out smoke"); the D51 followed on 24 Dec 1992, and he posted it to fj.sources, the source group of the Japanese Usenet hierarchy, on 2 Nov 1993. The joke is older: according to the Japanese Wikipedia it began on GCOS, was redone in awk with VT100 escape sequences, and a C version by Kazumasa Utashiro was posted to fj.sources in 1987. Until version 3.03 (1998) it had no delay, so the train ran at the speed of the line. The 1992 source does not survive: recorded by the collector in November 2026 from the current source (5.03, github.com/mtoyoda/sl), unchanged, one pass of the train for each variant, on a 24 x 80 vt100 at 9600 baud.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

A joke program for those who type sl instead of ls; no option: the D51 and its smoke cross the screen from right to left. One pass of the train on a 24 x 80 vt100 at 9600 baud, from start to exit: 57,973 bytes. The version history in sl.c starts with 1.00 on 11 Dec 1992, and he posted it to fj.sources (Japanese Usenet) on 2 Nov 1993; the joke itself is older (GCOS; awk with VT100 escapes; a C version by Kazumasa Utashiro posted to fj.sources in 1987), according to the Japanese Wikipedia; sl had no delay until 3.03 (1998), so in 1992 the speed of the line was its speed, as in this file. The 1992 source does not survive; the drawing of 5.03 is later work on it (D51 since 3.00, 24 Dec 1992). Same bytes on every run. |

### `cjrandall/`: CJRandall's site (shieldwolf.demon.co.uk), 1998-2001

CJRandall, a regular of alt.ascii-art.animation and later the editor of the ASCII Art FAQ (v3), kept animation code on his site, from the Wayback Machine. His JavaScript animations of 1998-2000, converted by the collector in November 2026 frame by frame like web-js-1997-1999/: the first four of August 1998 (piston, the transparency experiment, skuz, flaaz), copter and pop (May 1999), bat (Nov 2000), and four of his colour "Method 5" pages (1999-2000, dHTML layers made with miK, Mic Barendsz) as examples, with the HTML colours as ANSI colours: miK's dog (cut at 80 columns), the flower-pot, Matthew Thomas's boomerang, the skull. No pauses: `play.sh` plays each at the speed that matches the page's delay. `cjr-unix1*.vt`: "Shieldwolf Unix Script #1" (5 Feb 1999), "a simple anim-projector ... (unix terminal-independent code)": a Bourne shell script that clears the screen with `tput` and echoes two frames of llizard's bird nine times. Recorded by the collector in November 2026 from the script, unchanged, on a 24 x 80 vt100 at 9600 baud, once with `/bin/sh` (a System V echo, as on the Solaris the author tested it on) and once with bash (whose echo keeps the bird as llizard drew it).

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`cjr-unix1.vt`](collections/cjrandall/cjr-unix1.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/cjrandall/cjr-unix1/) | Birdy (Shieldwolf Unix Script #1, /bin/sh) — CJRandall (script); llizard (ejm) (drawing) | 1999-02-05 ("Last Revised" on the page); this recording 2026-11-23 | [shieldwolf.demon.co.uk/…/unix1-sh.htm](https://web.archive.org/web/20000929233919/http://www.shieldwolf.demon.co.uk/download/unix1-sh.htm) | verified | 0:01 at 9600 | `frames`. A Bourne shell script, "a simple anim-projector ... (unix terminal-independent code)": tput clear, then two frames of a bird turning its head ("birdy - ejm"), nine times; the delay loop of two expr calls does not reach a VT file. Run with /bin/sh (dash), whose System V echo reads \\ as one backslash, as the Bourne shell of Solaris that the author tested it on did: the bird's third line is " \ instead of llizard's " \\. The bird is llizard's example in her JavaScript animation lesson (posted to alt.ascii-art on 24 Feb 1998, signed ejm98). On a 24 x 80 vt100 at 9600 baud: 922 bytes, about 1 second. Same bytes on every run. |

### `llizard/`: llizard (ejm): her JavaScript animations, 1997-1999

llizard ("llizard aka ejm", signed ejm97-ejm99) was among the most active posters of alt.ascii-art.animation and wrote the JavaScript animation lesson that others built on (CJRandall thanks her for it; geocities SoHo/Studios/9334, 1998-99). Her animations were on her web site, not in the newsgroup: the lizard that eats, laughs, juggles and fishes, the pianist, the horses, tango, valentine 98. She also kept there animations of others under their names (Matthew Thomas, Andrew Hood, Robert Haschart, bbunny, PeterPunk, Valery Silivanov, Joan Stark). Converted by the collector in November 2026 frame by frame, like web-js-1997-1999/, from the Wayback copies of crosswinds.net/~llizard/ascii-art/animation/ (2001). No pauses: `play.sh` plays each at the speed that matches the page's delay. Her flying monkey is usenet-1994-1999/monkey--905d66.vt.

Page title: "Boomerang (Javascript ASCII-animation by Matthew Thomas)". Created by Matthew Thomas "on 31 October"; llizard shows his 12 frames and, under them, her own version with "a few frames at the end for verisimilitude in the loop" (22 frames), both every 250 ms, at once and over and over. Here one under the other as on the page: one cycle of 33 s (132 frames, 20x9), after which both are back at their first frame. About 9600 baud. Kept on llizard's site under the artist's name. Close to usenet-1994-1999/boomer.vt (64% of the lines). llizard (ejm) made the extra frames of the second version. |

Page title: "hulahoop (Javascript ASCII-animation by llizard aka ejm)". Three hula-hoopers, 3 frames each, every 120 ms, played at once on the page and over and over; captions "complicated" and "exhausting" (ejm98). On the page they stand one under the other; here side by side (one under the other needs 25 lines or more), 59x10, one cycle of 3 frames. The page waited 120 ms between frames: about 57600 baud. One cycle lasts a third of a second. llizard thanks CJRandall for the code that plays several animations on one page. |

### `vtgraph-1992/`: vtgraph.zip and xmasvt.zip: files dated 1989-1992

Two zip files found in December 2004 (the source was not recorded). Most files are dated 27-28 Feb and 29 May 1992; xmas11.vt is dated 4 Dec 1989. Several are in no online collection.

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

The oldest file of vtgraph-1992/. Found in no online collection. |

Found in no online collection. |

### `kermit/`: The Kermit archive (kermit.columbia.edu/vt100)

The `vt100` folder of Columbia University's Kermit archive ("*.vt are VT100 demos, to be typed or cat'd to a VT100 or emulator screen"). It is no longer on kermitproject.org; the copy comes from discmaster.textfiles.com (Nov 2026).

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [`torture.vt`](collections/kermit/torture.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/kermit/torture/) | VT100 series Torture Test (Kermit version) — Joe Smith | 1985-05-08; file 2001-07-25 | [discmaster.textfiles.com/…/torture.vt](https://discmaster.textfiles.com/file/17903/kermit.columbia.edu.tar/kermit.columbia.edu/vt100/torture.vt) | verified | 0:03 at 9600 | `vt100`. Differs from the textfiles copy in four places (an SI after ESC[1;2;3;4q, one "$", the final scroll-region reset, a trailing newline); it looks corrected. Which is closer to the 1985 original is unknown. Also: torturet.vt (textfiles, 3189 bytes). |
| [`decdemo.vt`](collections/kermit/decdemo.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/kermit/decdemo/) | Digital's Electronic Store (demo) — Digital Equipment Corporation | 1989-09-15 (file date) | [discmaster.textfiles.com/…/decdemo.vt](https://discmaster.textfiles.com/file/17903/kermit.columbia.edu.tar/kermit.columbia.edu/vt100/decdemo.vt) | verified | 1:03 at 9600 | `vt100`. Not ASCII art: a capture of DEC's demo of its online store ("Effective August 21, 1989"), with menus and forms. |
| [`box+umlaute.vt`](collections/kermit/box+umlaute.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/kermit/box-umlaute/) | Box with line drawing and umlauts (test) — unknown | 2004-11-04 (file date) | [discmaster.textfiles.com/…/box+umlaute.vt](https://discmaster.textfiles.com/file/17903/kermit.columbia.edu.tar/kermit.columbia.edu/vt100/box+umlaute.vt) | verified | 0:03 at 1200 | `vt100`. Not an animation: a 328-byte terminal test. |

### `maistrelis/`: Kostas Maistrelis: the collector's own animations, 1994-2004

Drawn by hand in a text editor (DOS EDIT), frame by frame, with the frame number at the top left, and played with his own players: ASCIIANI for DOS (1996-98, compressed `.ANM` files) and asciianm for Linux (1998-2003, text files with an `ARGUMENTS:` line). On 12 Feb 1998 he posted ASCIIANI v 3.1 with four samples (BEAR, MO2, POLIS2 and RAIL, the train) to alt.ascii-art.animation: "ASCII ART ANIMATION PROGRAM with Samples. Please Comments. Have Fun!!!" ([asciiart.eu](https://www.asciiart.eu/archives/usenet/message/m7fb34c078b); the attached Asciianm.zip was removed there, like every uuencoded file). Converted to VT files by the collector in November 2026 the way the asciianm README suggests, `asciianm file.txt -ansi > file.vt` (the `.txt` is next to the `.vt`), one pass each: whole frames, each line cut to 79 columns, the cursor sent home between frames. `bear2` is not his own: a story in pictures by an unknown maker, which he made into frames in 1998. The players waited 100-600 ms between frames; a VT file has no pauses, so `play.sh` plays these fast, at 115200 baud. `maistrelis-sig.vt` is different: what the three lines of C at the bottom of his Usenet signature of February 1998 printed when compiled and run by the collector in 2026. Its pauses are 998 BEL characters after each step, kept as printed: it beeps.

| File | Title / creator | Date | Sources | Certainty | Plays | Notes |

The first 102 are those of ANT.ANM (ASCIIANI, DOS) and of ant.txt of 3 Feb 1998, which stop there; the other 14 are of 8 Oct 1998. Until 25 Nov 2026 the file here was made from ANT.ANM. No .ini, so the player defaults apply: 22 lines per frame, 100 ms. Frames numbered at the top left. At the original speed: about 176,000 baud. Not among the samples of the 12 Feb 1998 post. |

Signed "By Maistrelis Kostas". 269 frames of 22 lines, 130 ms. The text was changed in Dec 2004, so this file differs from the collector's trein.vt of 2002. At the original speed: about 135,000 baud. An earlier version, RAIL.ANM (= TREIN6T.ANM, 8 Oct 1997), was one of the samples of Asciianm.zip, which the author posted with his DOS player ASCIIANI v 3.1 to alt.ascii-art.animation on 12 Feb 1998 ("Ascii Animation Program.", [aeu:m7fb34c078b](https://www.asciiart.eu/archives/usenet/message/m7fb34c078b)). |
| [`maistrelis-sig.vt`](collections/maistrelis/maistrelis-sig.vt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/maistrelis/maistrelis-sig/) | Signature with a C program ("gcc_prog.") — Kostas Maistrelis | 1998-02-10 (SIG.TXT in Asciianm.zip); posted 1998-02-12 | [aeu:m7fb34c078b](https://www.asciiart.eu/archives/usenet/message/m7fb34c078b)<br>mailbox #23<br>mailbox #24 | verified | 0:04 at 1800000 | `text`. An animation for the reader to compile and run, the "c-code in .sigs" of the ASCII art FAQ. It builds the collector's email address of 1998 (no longer in use) one letter at a time, from right to left: each letter slides from the start of the line to its place, with carriage returns only, no escape sequences. The pauses are 998 BEL characters after each of the 632 steps: 630,736 of the 644,337 bytes. They are kept as printed, so on a terminal with a bell it beeps, as it did in 1998. The drafts of the signature (Dec 1997 to Jan 1998) and its two versions are kept by the collector. The collector plays it at 1,800,000 baud. |
| [`bear2.vt`](collections/maistrelis/bear2.vt)<br>[`.txt`](collections/maistrelis/bear2.txt)<br>[▶ play](https://maistrelis.com/ascii-art/vt100-animations/maistrelis/bear2/) | Winnie the Pooh - Stuck in Rabbit's Hole — unknown (story and pictures); made into frames by Kostas Maistrelis | 1998-02-08 | BEAR2.TXT (collector's copy, 8 Feb 1998)<br>story.asc (collector's copy, Feb 1998) | verified | 1:36 at 28800 | `vt100`. A story in pictures ("Center the screen and press the spacebar once for each screen") that the collector made into an animation: 32 pictures, each as many times as it needs to be read, 157 frames of 22 lines, 600 ms each (BEAR2.INI). The maker is "unknown" at the end of the story itself. Not found anywhere else (November 2026: the collector's mailboxes, asciiart.eu, the other archives, a web search). Strong language. At the original speed: about 29,000 baud. See FINDINGS.md. |
